                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | 1. | 0.000000 | 192.168.1.10 | 172.16.1.100 | TCP | 74 | 50000 > 80 [SYN] Seq=0 Win=64240 Len=0 MSS=1460 SACK_PERM=1 WS=256 TSval=309395669 TSecr=0 |
| 2. | 0.000067 | 172.16.1.100 | 192.168.1.10 | TCP | 74 | 80 > 50000 [SYN, ACK] Seq=0 Ack=1 Win=65535 Len=0 MSS=1460 SACK_PERM=1 WS=256 TSval=12345678 TSecr=309395669 |
| 3. | 0.000134 | 192.168.1.10 | 172.16.1.100 | TCP | 66 | 50000 > 80 [ACK] Seq=1 Ack=1 Win=64240 Len=0 TSval=309395669 TSecr=12345678 |
| 4. | 0.000200 | 192.168.1.10 | 172.16.1.100 | HTTP | 150 | GET /index.html HTTP/1.1 |
| 5. | 0.000250 | 172.16.1.100 | 192.168.1.10 | TCP | 66 | 80 > 50000 [ACK] Seq=1 Ack=85 Win=65535 Len=0 TSval=12345678 TSecr=309395669 |
| 6. | 0.000300 | 172.16.1.100 | 192.168.1.10 | HTTP | 500 | HTTP/1.1 200 OK (text/html) |

**Based on the snippet, answer the following:**
a)  What is the client's IP address and ephemeral port?
b)  What is the server's IP address and well-known port?
c)  How long did the TCP three-way handshake take (from SYN to final ACK)?
d)  What is the HTTP status code returned by the server for the GET request?

**Answer 6:**
a)  **Client's IP address and ephemeral port:** 192.168.1.10, port 50000. (Identified from the SYN packet's source and the subsequent ACK's source).
b)  **Server's IP address and well-known port:** 172.16.1.100, port 80. (Identified from the SYN packet's destination and the SYN-ACK's source).
c)  **TCP three-way handshake duration:** 0.000134 seconds. (Time of final ACK (packet 3) - Time of SYN (packet 1) = 0.000134 - 0.000000 = 0.000134).
d)  **HTTP status code:** 200 OK. (From packet 6, "HTTP/1.1 200 OK").

**Question 7:** You are analyzing a capture file and suspect there might be DNS resolution issues. Write a Wireshark display filter to show only DNS query and response packets that have a response code indicating an error (e.g., `NXDOMAIN`, `SERVFAIL`).

**Answer 7:**
```
dns.flags.response == 1 and dns.flags.rcode != 0
```
**Explanation:**
*   `dns.flags.response == 1`: Filters for DNS response packets (as opposed to queries).
*   `dns.flags.rcode != 0`: Filters for response packets where the Response Code (RCODE) is not 0 (which signifies "No Error"). Any other RCODE value indicates an error (e.g., 1 for `FORMERR`, 2 for `SERVFAIL`, 3 for `NXDOMAIN`).

**Partial Credit Guidance:** `dns.flags.rcode == 3` (for NXDOMAIN specifically) would get partial credit, but the broader filter is more comprehensive.

**Question 8:** You are monitoring network traffic and want to identify if any host is performing a high number of ARP requests, which could indicate an ARP cache poisoning attempt or a misconfigured device. Describe how you would use Wireshark's **Conversations** statistics (specifically the "Ethernet" tab) to quickly identify such a host.

**Answer 8:**
To identify a host performing a high number of ARP requests using Wireshark's Conversations statistics:
1.  Go to `Statistics > Conversations`.
2.  Navigate to the **Ethernet** tab within the Conversations window.
3.  **Filter for ARP:** In the "Filter" box at the bottom of the Conversations window, type `arp` and press Enter. This will filter the conversations list to show only Ethernet conversations that include ARP traffic.
4.  **Sort by Packets (A->B or B->A):** Click on the "Pkts (A->B)" or "Pkts (B->A)" column header to sort the conversations by the number of packets sent from one endpoint to another.
5.  **Analyze:** Look for entries where one MAC address (the "A" or "B" column) has an unusually high number of ARP packets associated with it, especially if it's consistently initiating many ARP requests (e.g., `Pkts (A->B)` is very high for a specific source MAC). This could indicate a device excessively querying for MAC addresses, which might be a symptom of a problem. You can then select that conversation and click "Follow Stream" or "Go To Packet" to examine the specific ARP packets.

### Section 3: Wireshark Filter Writing & Analysis (4 Questions)

**Question 9:** Write a Wireshark display filter to show all HTTP traffic where the User-Agent header indicates a mobile device (e.g., "Android", "iPhone", "Mobile").

**Answer 9:**
```
http.user_agent contains "Android" or http.user_agent contains "iPhone" or http.user_agent contains "Mobile"
```
**Explanation:**
*   `http.user_agent`: Targets the HTTP User-Agent header field.
*   `contains`: A string operator that checks if a substring is present within the field.
*   `"Android"`, `"iPhone"`, `"Mobile"`: Common substrings found in mobile device User-Agent strings. The `or` operator combines these conditions, showing traffic if any of them are true.

**Partial Credit Guidance:** A filter for just one of the mobile agents would receive partial credit.

**Question 10:** You need to capture traffic on a specific interface (`eth0`) but only for TCP connections originating from IP address `192.168.1.10` and destined for port `443` (HTTPS). Write the **capture filter** you would use.

**Answer 10:**
```
tcp src host 192.168.1.10 and dst port 443
```
**Explanation:**
*   `tcp`: Specifies the TCP protocol.
*   `src host 192.168.1.10`: Filters for packets where the source IP address is 192.168.1.10.
*   `dst port 443`: Filters for packets where the destination port is 443.
*   `and`: Combines the conditions, requiring both to be true.

**Common Mistake:** Using Wireshark display filter syntax (`tcp.src == 192.168.1.10`) instead of BPF syntax for a capture filter.

**Question 11:** Write a Wireshark display filter to identify potential large file transfers over FTP. Specifically, filter for FTP data connections where the data segment length (payload) is greater than 1024 bytes.

**Answer 11:**
```
ftp-data and tcp.len > 1024
```
**Explanation:**
*   `ftp-data`: This is a Wireshark protocol abbreviation that identifies packets belonging to the FTP data channel (typically on ephemeral ports, not port 20/21).
*   `tcp.len`: Represents the length of the TCP payload (the data segment, excluding TCP/IP headers).
*   `> 1024`: Filters for packets where the TCP payload length is greater than 1024 bytes, indicating a substantial data transfer.

**Question 12:** You suspect a specific server (IP: `10.0.0.50`) is experiencing high latency when responding to client requests. Write a Wireshark display filter to show only TCP packets where the **Round Trip Time (RTT)** from the client to this server and back (SYN to SYN-ACK) is greater than 200 milliseconds.

**Answer 12:**
```
tcp.analysis.initial_rtt > 0.200 and ip.dst == 10.0.0.50
```
**Explanation:**
*   `tcp.analysis.initial_rtt`: This Wireshark field specifically calculates the RTT for the initial SYN-SYN/ACK handshake. It's a powerful metric for assessing baseline network latency.
*   `> 0.200`: Filters for RTT values greater than 200 milliseconds (Wireshark expresses this in seconds).
*   `ip.dst == 10.0.0.50`: Ensures we are looking at RTTs where the server `10.0.0.50` is the destination of the initial SYN.

**Partial Credit Guidance:** A filter that attempts to calculate RTT manually using `frame.time_delta` between SYN and SYN-ACK would show understanding but is less precise and efficient than `tcp.analysis.initial_rtt`.

---

### Section 4: Design & Debugging Problems (3 Questions)

**Question 13:** A user reports that they can't access a specific website, but other users on the same network can. You have Wireshark installed on the user's machine. Outline the steps you would take to capture relevant traffic and use Wireshark to diagnose the problem. What specific Wireshark features would you focus on?

**Answer 13:**
**Steps to Diagnose:**
1.  **Preparation:** Ask the user to reproduce the issue while you are ready to capture. Clear the browser's DNS cache and regular cache to ensure a fresh attempt.
2.  **Capture Filter:** Start a capture on the user's machine, using a capture filter to narrow down the traffic. A good starting point would be `host <website_IP_address> or dns` (if you know the website's IP) or `port 53 or port 80 or port 443` to cover DNS and web traffic. This minimizes irrelevant data.
3.  **Reproduce Issue:** Instruct the user to attempt accessing the problematic website.
4.  **Stop Capture:** Stop the capture once the error is clearly observed.
5.  **Initial Analysis (Display Filters):**
    *   **DNS Check:** Apply `dns` display filter. Look for the DNS query for the website. Is there a response? Is it `NXDOMAIN`? Is the IP address correct? If no DNS response, it could be a local DNS client issue or an upstream DNS server problem.
    *   **TCP Handshake Check:** If DNS resolves correctly, filter for `ip.addr == <website_IP_address> and tcp.port == 80 or tcp.port == 443`. Look for the TCP three-way handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK).
        *   **Missing SYN-ACK/RST:** If the SYN is sent but no SYN-ACK or a RST is received, it indicates a firewall blocking, server down, or incorrect port.
        *   **Delayed SYN-ACK:** High `tcp.analysis.initial_rtt` suggests network latency or server load.
    *   **HTTP/HTTPS Check:** If TCP handshake is successful, filter for `http` or `tls`.
        *   **HTTP:** Look for the GET request and the server's response. Is it a 4xx client error (e.g., 403 Forbidden, 404 Not Found) or a 5xx server error?
        *   **HTTPS (TLS):** Look for the TLS handshake. Are there any alerts? Is the certificate exchange successful? If the TLS handshake fails, it could be a certificate issue or a cipher mismatch.
6.  **Expert Information:** Check `Analyze > Expert Information` for any critical or error messages related to TCP (retransmissions, out-of-order) or application protocols.
7.  **Follow TCP Stream:** If a connection is established, use "Follow TCP Stream" to see the full conversation in plain text (for HTTP) or encrypted bytes (for HTTPS), which can reveal application-layer issues.

**Specific Wireshark Features to Focus On:**
*   **Capture Filters (BPF):** For efficient data collection.
*   **Display Filters:** `dns`, `tcp.flags.syn`, `tcp.flags.reset`, `http`, `tls`, `ip.addr == <specific_IP>`.
*   **Time Column/Delta Time:** To measure delays between packets.
*   **Expert Information:** For quick identification of network anomalies.
*   **Follow TCP Stream:** To reconstruct application-layer conversations.
*   **I/O Graphs:** To visualize traffic patterns over time, especially if the issue is intermittent.

**Question 14:** You are asked to verify if a new web server (IP: `192.168.1.200`) is correctly configured to serve content over HTTPS. You have access to a network tap that mirrors all traffic to and from this server. Describe how you would use Wireshark to confirm the HTTPS configuration, focusing on the TLS handshake and certificate exchange.

**Answer 14:**
**Steps to Verify HTTPS Configuration:**
1.  **Capture Setup:** Connect your Wireshark machine to the network tap (SPAN port) that mirrors traffic to/from `192.168.1.200`. Ensure your NIC is in promiscuous mode.
2.  **Capture Filter:** Start a capture using a capture filter like `host 192.168.1.200 and port 443` to focus only on HTTPS traffic to/from the server.
3.  **Test Access:** From a client machine, attempt to access the web server over HTTPS (e.g., `https://192.168.1.200/`). Make sure to trigger a new connection, not one from a cached session.
4.  **Stop Capture:** Stop the capture after a successful or failed connection attempt.
5.  **TLS Handshake Analysis (Display Filter: `tls`):**
    *   **Client Hello:** Look for the `Client Hello` message. Verify the client is proposing acceptable TLS versions and cipher suites.
    *   **Server Hello:** Look for the `Server Hello` message. Verify the server responds with a compatible TLS version and cipher suite.
    *   **Certificate:** The server should send its `Certificate` message.
        *   **Verify Certificate Chain:** In the Packet Details pane, expand the `Transport Layer Security` layer, then the `Handshake Protocol: Certificate` section. You should see the server's certificate and potentially its chain (intermediate CAs, root CA).
        *   **Check Certificate Details:** Inspect the certificate's details:
            *   **Common Name (CN) / Subject Alternative Name (SAN):** Does it match the server's hostname or IP address?
            *   **Validity Period:** Is the certificate currently valid (not expired or not yet valid)?
            *   **Issuer:** Is it issued by a trusted Certificate Authority?
            *   **Key Usage:** Is it appropriate for server authentication?
    *   **Server Key Exchange (if applicable):** If using DHE/ECDHE, the server will send its public key.
    *   **Server Hello Done:** Indicates the server has sent all its handshake messages.
    *   **Client Key Exchange, Change Cipher Spec, Encrypted Handshake Message:** The client's part of the handshake.
    *   **Application Data:** After a successful handshake, you should see `Application Data` packets, indicating encrypted HTTP traffic.
6.  **Troubleshooting TLS Alerts:** If the handshake fails, look for `TLS Alert` messages. Common alerts include:
    *   `Handshake Failure`: Generic failure.
    *   `Bad Certificate`: Problem with the server's certificate.
    *   `Unsupported Certificate`: Client doesn't trust the CA or doesn't support the certificate type.
    *   `Protocol Version`: Mismatch in TLS versions.
    *   `Cipher Suite`: Mismatch in supported cipher suites.
7.  **Expert Information:** Check for any TLS-related warnings or errors flagged by Wireshark's Expert Information.

**Confirmation of Correct Configuration:** A correctly configured HTTPS server will show a complete and successful TLS handshake, with a valid certificate chain, appropriate TLS versions and cipher suites agreed upon, and subsequent application data flowing encrypted.

**Question 15:** A network administrator reports that users are experiencing slow file transfers over SMB (Server Message Block) to a Windows file server. You've captured traffic between a client and the server. Describe how you would use Wireshark to investigate this performance issue, focusing on identifying common SMB-related bottlenecks.

**Answer 15:**
**Steps to Investigate SMB Performance:**
1.  **Capture Traffic:** Obtain a packet capture during a slow file transfer between a client and the SMB server. A capture filter like `host <client_IP> and host <server_IP> and port 445` (for SMB) would be appropriate.
2.  **Initial Overview (I/O Graphs):**
    *   Go to `Statistics > I/O Graphs`.
    *   Plot the `Bytes/Tick` to visualize the throughput. Look for drops or inconsistencies in the data rate. This gives a high-level view of the transfer speed.
    *   Plot `Packets/Tick` to see the packet rate.
3.  **SMB Protocol Hierarchy:**
    *   Go to `Statistics > Protocol Hierarchy`.
    *   Examine the percentage of traffic dedicated to SMB. This confirms SMB is indeed the primary protocol.
4.  **SMB Expert Information:**
    *   Go to `Analyze > Expert Information`. Look for any SMB-specific warnings or errors. Wireshark can flag issues like "SMB: Multiple SMB responses for request," "SMB: Duplicate SMB response," or "SMB: Out-of-order SMB response," which can indicate server-side processing issues or network problems.
5.  **SMB Read/Write Analysis (Display Filters):**
    *   **`smb.opcode == 0x06` (SMB Read) and `smb.opcode == 0x0A` (SMB Write):** Filter for read and write requests/responses.
    *   **`smb.time`:** Wireshark often calculates the response time for SMB operations. Look for unusually high values in this field. Sort by this column to identify slow operations.
    *   **TCP Retransmissions (`tcp.analysis.retransmission`):** Filter for retransmissions. High retransmission rates indicate network congestion, packet loss, or duplex mismatches, which severely impact SMB performance.
    *   **Window Full (`tcp.analysis.flags` and `tcp.window_size == 0`):** Look for "TCP Window Full" conditions or `tcp.window_size == 0` packets. This means the receiver's buffer is full, and it cannot accept more data, forcing the sender to wait. This points to a slow receiver or application processing bottleneck.
    *   **Zero Window (`tcp.window_size == 0`):** Similar to Window Full, a zero window indicates the receiver is temporarily unable to process more data.
    *   **Large Delays between SMB Requests/Responses:** Use the `Time` column and `Delta time from previous displayed packet` to identify long gaps between an SMB request and its corresponding response. This could indicate server processing delays or application-layer issues.
6.  **TCP Stream Graphs:**
    *   Use `Statistics > TCP Stream Graphs > Throughput Graph` to visualize the actual data transfer rate over time for specific SMB connections.
    *   Use `Statistics > TCP Stream Graphs > Window Scaling Graph` to see how TCP window sizes are being managed, which can help diagnose window-related issues.
7.  **SMB Version:** Check the SMB version being used (`smb.dialect`). Older versions (SMBv1) are less efficient and less secure than SMBv2/SMBv3.

**Common SMB-Related Bottlenecks to Look For:**
*   **High TCP Retransmissions:** Network layer issue.
*   **TCP Window Full/Zero Window:** Receiver processing bottleneck or network congestion.
*   **High SMB Response Times (`smb.time`):** Server-side processing delay or disk I/O bottleneck.
*   **SMB Protocol Overhead:** Inefficient use of SMB commands.
*   **SMB Version:** Older SMB versions are less performant.
*   **Network Latency:** High RTTs will naturally slow down any chatty protocol like SMB.

---

## Course Conclusion

You have successfully completed the Wireshark Certified Network Analyst (WCNA) course! This journey has equipped you with a powerful and indispensable skill set for anyone working with computer networks. You are no longer just observing network traffic; you are now capable of dissecting it, understanding its intricate dance, and extracting meaningful insights.

Specifically, you can now:
*   Confidently **capture network traffic** using Wireshark and `tshark`, applying precise capture filters to target relevant data.
*   **Navigate and customize the Wireshark interface** to efficiently analyze packet captures.
*   **Master Wireshark's robust display filter syntax** to isolate, categorize, and deeply inspect specific packets and conversations.
*   **Perform detailed protocol analysis** for foundational protocols like Ethernet, IP, TCP, and UDP, as well as common application protocols such as HTTP, DNS, and SMB.
*   **Utilize Wireshark's statistical tools** (I/O Graphs, Protocol Hierarchy, Conversations, Endpoints) to gain high-level overviews and identify trends.
*   **Troubleshoot common network performance issues** by analyzing TCP retransmissions, windowing, latency, and application response times.
*   **Investigate basic network security incidents** by identifying suspicious traffic patterns, unauthorized access attempts, and potential data exfiltration.
*   **Document your findings** clearly and concisely, providing actionable recommendations based on your network analysis.

These are not just theoretical concepts; they are practical, hands-on skills that will make you an invaluable asset in any IT or cybersecurity role.

### Where to go next

Your journey as a network analyst is just beginning! To continue building on this strong foundation, consider these next steps and resources:

1.  **Pursue WCNA Certification:** The official Wireshark Certified Network Analyst certification is the natural next step. This course has prepared you well for the exam, and obtaining the certification will formally validate your expertise.
2.  **Advanced Wireshark Techniques:** Explore more advanced features of Wireshark, such as custom dissectors, scripting with Lua, or integrating Wireshark with other analysis tools.
3.  **Deep Dive into Specific Protocols:** Choose a protocol that interests you (e.g., VoIP/SIP, Industrial Protocols like Modbus/TCP, specific routing protocols) and perform a deep, self-directed analysis using Wireshark.
4.  **Network Security Specialization:** Combine your Wireshark skills with knowledge of network security principles, intrusion detection systems (IDS), and security information and event management (SIEM) tools to pursue a career as a Network Security Analyst.
5.  **Cloud Networking:** Apply your packet analysis skills to cloud environments. While direct packet capture can be challenging in the cloud, understanding network flows and logs still benefits immensely from a packet-level perspective.
6.  **Join the Community:** Engage with the Wireshark community forums, attend webinars, or follow network analysis blogs. Learning from others and sharing your experiences is a fantastic way to grow.

The world of networking is constantly evolving, but the fundamental principles of packet analysis remain timeless. Continue to practice, experiment, and question what you see on the wire. Every packet tells a story, and you now have the skills to read them. We wish you the best in your continued learning and career!
